### S1Q08F · followup · 12 words · clip 5.8 s · first detection +0.0 s from the end of the clip · coverage 100 %

> How could repeatedly training on customers affected by previous campaigns introduce bias?

**In-app answer 1** — whisper, +1.1 s → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)
_grader: Correctly explains the feedback loop where treated customers look low-risk._

heard: "How could repeatedly training on customers affected by previous campaigns introd"

> Training on past data where campaigns were active creates a feedback loop that distorts true customer behavior. If an incentive successfully kept a high-risk customer from churning, the model learns that this profile stays, even though they would have left without the intervention. That means future predictions end up measuring response to marketing rather than baseline churn risk.

_grader: Correctly explains intervention confounding and the random control remedy._

> It creates a feedback loop where the model learns to predict the previous model's targeting logic rather than actual customer churn. If we only train on customers who received a campaign, we're effectively training on a biased sample where the outcome is confounded by our own intervention. To fix this, I use propensity score matching or hold out a random control group that receives no intervention to establish a true baseline for churn probability.

### S1Q10F · followup · 23 words · clip 10.0 s · first detection +1.8 s from the end of the clip · coverage 100 %

> The application receives an authorization error after deployment. How would you distinguish an identity problem from a permissions problem or a network problem?

**In-app answer 1** — whisper, +1.8 s → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)
_grader: Clear diagnostic ladder: token acquisition, then 403, then connectivity._

heard: "The application receives an authorization error after deployment. How would you "

> I start by checking token acquisition to rule out an identity problem; if the managed identity cannot fetch a valid token, the client configuration or MSI endpoint is misconfigured. If the token succeeds but returns a forbidden response, it's a permissions problem, meaning the identity lacks the required Azure RBAC role or database-level grants. If the request times out or fails to connect entirely, it's a network problem caused by blocked VNet integration, missing private endpoints, or restrictive firewall rules.

> To isolate contributions, I run a sequential ablation study by freezing the upstream components and measuring the downstream impact. I’d start by swapping the OCR engine or the retrieval strategy while keeping the agent orchestration and prompting constant, then measure the delta in our F1 score. By isolating one variable at a time—like comparing a baseline retriever against our hybrid approach—I can quantify exactly how much each layer contributes to the final accuracy.
> 
> For LLM judge bias, I use a three-pronged validation strategy. First, I compare judge scores against a smaller, high-quality human-annotated gold set to calculate the correlation coefficient. Second, I perform position-bias testing by swapping the order of the model's output and the reference text to ensure the judge doesn't favor the first option. Finally, I use a stronger model, like a Claude 3.5 Sonnet or GPT-4o, as a meta-judge to audit a random sample of the primary judge's decisions.

> When does an agent justify its complexity? Consider a question that requires evidence from three documents, and compare a fixed retrieval workflow, query decomposition, and an agent that chooses tools dynamically.

heard: "When does an agent justify its complexity? Consider a question that requires evi"

> An agent justifies its complexity when the retrieval path depends on intermediate results—meaning document two's lookup requires an entity extracted from document one. A fixed retrieval workflow fails here because it fetches documents blindly without adapting to prior findings. Query decomposition solves multi-hop needs by breaking the question into sequential sub-queries, which works well if the document structure is predictable. However, when the task requires conditional tool selection—like deciding whether to query a vector database, parse a financial table, or re-route based on extraction gaps—a dynamic agent justifies its overhead by reasoning through those conditional branches adaptively.

### S2Q04F · followup · 24 words · clip 8.0 s · first detection −1.8 s from the end of the clip · coverage 100 %

> Why might you use rank fusion instead of adding the raw scores, and how would you add a configurable weight for each retrieval source?

**In-app answer 1** — whisper, +1.1 s → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 2)
_grader: Correct scale-mismatch rationale and per-source weight multiplier._

heard: "Why might you use Rank Fusion instead of adding the raw scores, and how would yo"

> Raw scores from keyword and vector searches live on completely different scales, so adding them directly lets whichever model outputs larger numbers dominate the results. Reciprocal Rank Fusion avoids this by looking only at the document's position rather than its raw score, creating a universal ranking scale. To make that configurable, I multiply each retriever's reciprocal rank term by an explicit weight coefficient before summing them up across both lists for each document.

**Arm gemini-3.1-flash-lite_captured-low-r2** — TTFT 6.0 s, 91 words → **acceptable** (correctness 2 · on_topic 2 · delivery 1)
_grader: Correct scale-mismatch rationale and per-source weighting; slightly long._

> Raw scores are often incomparable because vector similarity and keyword scores exist on completely different scales. If you just sum them, the source with the larger range effectively drowns out the other. Rank Fusion ignores the absolute values and focuses on the relative position, which is a common denominator. To add weights, I simply multiply the reciprocal rank of each source by a configurable coefficient—like 0.7 for vector and 0.3 for keyword—before summing them. This gives me granular control over which retrieval method I trust more for a specific query type.

> Imagine a multi-hop query where Document A has two high-relevance chunks that max out our budget, but the critical connecting fact needed for the second hop is in Document B, which gets squeezed out because its individual relevance score is slightly lower. To fix that poor coverage, I switch from pure greedy relevance to a Maximal Marginal Relevance scoring function that penalizes redundancy and rewards document diversity. For multi-hop dependencies, I use an iterative retrieval loop where the first-hop evidence explicitly seeds the next query to pull in structurally connected downstream chunks.

> A greedy policy fails when top results are redundant—for example, if you're asking about quarterly revenue trends and all your top chunks come from the same Q3 report, you miss the Q4 data entirely. For multi-hop dependencies, I move away from single-pass retrieval. I use agentic workflows, like the ones I built with LangGraph, to break the query into sub-questions. The agent retrieves for the first part, evaluates if the answer is complete, and then triggers a second, targeted retrieval step if more context is needed.
